I don’t want to do anything, I don’t want to act on it, but having those thoughts at all really frightened me.\n\nDoctor: Did these thoughts and the way you’ve been feeling start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start around a particular change. It’s been about… a year and seven months now, I think. Around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it like that, because it’s just curriculum and spreadsheets and meetings, but there were so many extra pieces—like people needing updates, and everyone having opinions, and I’d be trying to grade after dinner and then remember some rubric thing at midnight. Sorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, that’s when it started, or at least when I really noticed it beginning.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you felt this way most days for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, definitely. It’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, since I took over that STEM assessment redesign along with my usual teaching load. I keep thinking, “Oh, it’ll settle after this deadline,” but then there’s another meeting or another set of spreadsheets, and somehow it’s just… kept going.\n\nDoctor: If that school project were taken off your plate, do you think you would still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I keep trying to imagine that, like if someone just took the whole binder and the meetings and all the tracking sheets away, whether I’d sort of… snap back.\n\nPart of me thinks I’d feel lighter, obviously. But I’m not sure it would all just disappear. It’s hard to separate it now, because it’s been tied to my whole routine for so long. Even on weekends or breaks, I catch myself still feeling like something is hanging over me. But then again, teachers are terrible at resting—at least the ones I know. We’ll get a day off and spend it reorganizing cupboards or answering emails we swore we wouldn’t answer.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I think it might help, but I don’t know if it would fix everything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from school responsibilities for a while, do you still feel this low?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, when I’m physically away from school—like not in the building, not doing meetings—it doesn’t always switch off in my head.\n\nBut then sometimes I wonder if I’m just bad at being “away,” because I’ll be at home and notice the tote bag with papers in it, or I’ll remember some email I haven’t answered, and then suddenly it’s like I’m back there anyway. My partner says teachers have a weird definition of time off, which is probably true… we had a whole conversation once about how summer “break” is not really a break, and then I started making a list of classroom supplies, which kind of proved the point.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can say clearly. I think I can still feel low even when I’m not actively doing school stuff, but I’m not sure how much of that is because it’s still in the background.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like during uni when everything felt dramatic and impossible for about five minutes—well, it probably wasn’t five minutes, but you know what I mean. And teaching has never exactly been a calm profession, so there have definitely been times I’ve felt really worn down.\n\nBut like this? I’m not sure. I can’t think of another time that stands out clearly in the same way. It’s hard because you look back and everything gets sort of blurry, and I start comparing it to exam periods or family stress or my first year teaching, which was its own little disaster. So… I don’t know if I’d say I had another period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing school-related is happening, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I want to say yes, sometimes, but then I start picking apart whether “nothing school-related” ever really happens, because my brain will make something school-related out of almost anything. Like I’ll be watching some completely unrelated programme and suddenly think, “Oh, that would make a good example for Year 10,” and then there I am again.\n\nBut if you mean, like, if I’m just sitting there and no one’s emailed me and I’m not planning anything… I think I can still feel low. I just don’t know how cleanly I can separate it out. It’s all a bit tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about lots of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lots of different things,” exactly. I do worry, but then I get stuck trying to decide whether it’s excessive or just… being a person with responsibilities.\n\nLike, I can worry about practical things, and then it sort of spirals into remembering something completely unrelated—like I’ll think about paying a bill and then suddenly I’m wondering whether I replied to someone’s message from three days ago. But I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m worrying about everything.” It’s more… bits and pieces, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if I’d say “many different parts.” I mean, I do worry, but then I start making categories in my head, which is probably not what you’re asking. Work sort of leaks into everything, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m worrying about life in general or just one thing wearing different hats.\n\nLike, I’ll be thinking about something ordinary—shopping, or whether I’ve got clean clothes for tomorrow—and then somehow it becomes connected to school, because I need to remember something for a lesson, or I’ve forgotten to print something, and then I’m off on that track. But I don’t sit there with a list of worries about every area of my life, if that makes sense. I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: During these past year and seven months, have you felt down or low nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can honestly say nearly every day, because then I start thinking, well, what counts as “down”? Like, is it the whole day, or waking up with that sort of grey feeling, or just dipping in and out?\n\nIt has been around a lot, I think. But I haven’t been marking it on a calendar or anything. And the “past year and seven months” is such a specific stretch that my brain immediately wants to break it into terms and holidays and exam periods, which probably isn’t helpful. I can say it’s felt frequent, but I’m not sure I could confidently say nearly every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things sometimes, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m interested. But it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and messing about in the garden—there was always something to check on, or weed, or decide whether I’d planted too many tomatoes again. And I help with the robotics club at school too, which used to be one of those things that made the week feel a bit brighter.\n\nNow I mostly just… go through the motions. Or I cancel. I’ll tell myself I’m too busy, or that someone else can cover it, but if I’m honest, it’s more that none of it feels enjoyable in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt down or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: Two years… I’m not sure I can pin it down that neatly. I keep trying to line it up with school years, because that’s how I remember time—autumn term, mock exams, summer holidays—and then it gets a bit muddled.\n\nI have felt low a lot, but “most of the time” over two whole years feels hard to answer. Some stretches blur together, and then there are bits where I was just busy or distracted, or I don’t know if I’d have called it depressed exactly. So… I don’t think I can say for certain.\n\nDoctor: If the school workload were no longer a problem, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Part of me wants to say yes, because the workload is such an obvious thing to blame, isn’t it? Like marking piles up and suddenly everything feels impossible.\n\nBut then… I don’t know. Even in lighter weeks, I can’t always tell whether I actually feel better or whether I’m just less cornered. It’s hard to separate it out. Teaching kind of leaks into everything—weekends, evenings, even when I’m buying milk I’m thinking about whether Year 10 understood the lesson.\n\nSo if the workload disappeared completely… I don’t know. Maybe I’d feel better. Maybe I’d still feel low and just have fewer excuses for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep’s been pretty rubbish, actually. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open while I’m brushing my teeth, and then as soon as I’m in bed my brain starts doing this awful replay of everything.\n\nIt’ll be assessment deadlines, or some tiny mistake I made in class, or whether I handled something badly with a pupil—things that probably no one else is even thinking about. And then I’ll wake up around 4 in the morning and just… lie there. Sometimes I doze a bit, but I don’t really get properly back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I decided to eat less, or anything like that. It’s more that on school days I’ll get to, I don’t know, three or four o’clock and realise I’ve only had coffee or maybe half a cereal bar that was in my bag.\n\nSometimes I tell myself I’ll eat at break, and then there’s a student at the door, or someone needs the photocopier code, or I’m trying to mark something quickly, and then lunch just sort of… disappears. Our staffroom fridge is also disgusting, which doesn’t help—people leave yoghurts in there for weeks.\n\nBut yes, my appetite’s definitely changed. I’m not really trying to lose weight, but over the last year my clothes have got noticeably looser. At first I thought maybe things had stretched in the wash, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but… yeah, it’s enough that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… quite a lot, actually. It’s embarrassing because teaching is basically being able to make decisions every thirty seconds, isn’t it? And I used to be quick with marking, not necessarily cheerful about it, but quick.\n\nNow I’ll read the same student answer three or four times and still sit there thinking, “What mark is that?” Like I can see the words, but they don’t… settle into meaning properly. And then I start second-guessing the mark scheme, and whether I’m being fair, and whether I’m too harsh on that particular class because they were awful after lunch on Tuesdays—sorry, that’s probably not relevant.\n\nEven simple things can take forever. I had to order lab supplies the other day—just basic stuff—and I got completely stuck choosing what to get. I think I spent nearly an hour going back and forth over it, which is ridiculous. It’s like my brain just won’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m speaking slowly or anything.\n\nMoving slowly… I mean, mornings at school can feel like wading through treacle, but that might just be because the photocopier jams every other day and Year 9 always seem to appear before I’ve even taken my coat off. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI suppose I haven’t noticed myself being noticeably slower in a way other people would point out. I might feel slow inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I look or sound slower.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to look forward to softball, book club, my garden, and robotics club, but now I mostly go through the motions or cancel because none of it feels enjoyable anymore.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: Most school days I forget or avoid eating until late afternoon, and over the past year my clothes have gotten noticeably looser even though I’m not trying to lose weight.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: I can be exhausted and still lie awake replaying assessment deadlines and classroom mistakes, then wake up around 4 a.m. and never really get back to sleep.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: By the time I get through teaching and a department meeting, my body feels so heavy that even making dinner or answering a few parent emails feels like climbing a hill.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; I’ve also stopped showing up for friends and family the way I used to, so most of my life has narrowed to barely getting through school and then going home exhausted.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 7 months, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’m not really trying to lose weight, but over the last year my clothes have got noticeably looser. At first I thought maybe things had stretched in the wash, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but… yeah, it’s enough that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… quite a lot, actually. It’s embarrassing because teaching is basically being able to make decisions every thirty seconds, isn’t it? And I used to be quick with marking, not necessarily cheerful about it, but quick.\n\nNow I’ll read the same student answer three or four times and still sit there thinking, “What mark is that?” Like I can see the words, but they don’t… settle into meaning properly. And then I start second-guessing the mark scheme, and whether I’m being fair, and whether I’m too harsh on that particular class because they were awful after lunch on Tuesdays—sorry, that’s probably not relevant.\n\nEven simple things can take forever. I had to order lab supplies the other day—just basic stuff—and I got completely stuck choosing what to get. I think I spent nearly an hour going back and forth over it, which is ridiculous. It’s like my brain just won’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m speaking slowly or anything.\n\nMoving slowly… I mean, mornings at school can feel like wading through treacle, but that might just be because the photocopier jams every other day and Year 9 always seem to appear before I’ve even taken my coat off. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI suppose I haven’t noticed myself being noticeably slower in a way other people would point out. I might feel slow inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I look or sound slower.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling unusually tired or drained of energy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",

Yes. By the time I’ve got through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting, I feel completely wrung out. Like my body’s heavy, if that makes sense.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F32.9, F43.2, F41.1, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I… um, I’m not even sure how to start. I guess I’m here because I scared myself. I’m a teacher, and usually I can kind of keep it together for the kids—well, my students, I mean, they notice everything, honestly—but lately on the drive home I’ve caught myself thinking that maybe everyone would be better off without me.\n\nAnd sometimes at night I think it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. I don’t want to do anything, I don’t want to act on it, but having those thoughts at all really frightened me.\n\nDoctor: Did these thoughts and the way you’ve been feeling start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start around a particular change. It’s been about… a year and seven months now, I think. Around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it like that, because it’s just curriculum and spreadsheets and meetings, but there were so many extra pieces—like people needing updates, and everyone having opinions, and I’d be trying to grade after dinner and then remember some rubric thing at midnight. Sorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, that’s when it started, or at least when I really noticed it beginning.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you felt this way most days for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, definitely. It’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, since I took over that STEM assessment redesign along with my usual teaching load. I keep thinking, “Oh, it’ll settle after this deadline,” but then there’s another meeting or another set of spreadsheets, and somehow it’s just… kept going.\n\nDoctor: If that school project were taken off your plate, do you think you would still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I keep trying to imagine that, like if someone just took the whole binder and the meetings and all the tracking sheets away, whether I’d sort of… snap back.\n\nPart of me thinks I’d feel lighter, obviously. But I’m not sure it would all just disappear. It’s hard to separate it now, because it’s been tied to my whole routine for so long. Even on weekends or breaks, I catch myself still feeling like something is hanging over me. But then again, teachers are terrible at resting—at least the ones I know. We’ll get a day off and spend it reorganizing cupboards or answering emails we swore we wouldn’t answer.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I think it might help, but I don’t know if it would fix everything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from school responsibilities for a while, do you still feel this low?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, when I’m physically away from school—like not in the building, not doing meetings—it doesn’t always switch off in my head.\n\nBut then sometimes I wonder if I’m just bad at being “away,” because I’ll be at home and notice the tote bag with papers in it, or I’ll remember some email I haven’t answered, and then suddenly it’s like I’m back there anyway. My partner says teachers have a weird definition of time off, which is probably true… we had a whole conversation once about how summer “break” is not really a break, and then I started making a list of classroom supplies, which kind of proved the point.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can say clearly. I think I can still feel low even when I’m not actively doing school stuff, but I’m not sure how much of that is because it’s still in the background.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like during uni when everything felt dramatic and impossible for about five minutes—well, it probably wasn’t five minutes, but you know what I mean. And teaching has never exactly been a calm profession, so there have definitely been times I’ve felt really worn down.\n\nBut like this? I’m not sure. I can’t think of another time that stands out clearly in the same way. It’s hard because you look back and everything gets sort of blurry, and I start comparing it to exam periods or family stress or my first year teaching, which was its own little disaster. So… I don’t know if I’d say I had another period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing school-related is happening, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I want to say yes, sometimes, but then I start picking apart whether “nothing school-related” ever really happens, because my brain will make something school-related out of almost anything. Like I’ll be watching some completely unrelated programme and suddenly think, “Oh, that would make a good example for Year 10,” and then there I am again.\n\nBut if you mean, like, if I’m just sitting there and no one’s emailed me and I’m not planning anything… I think I can still feel low. I just don’t know how cleanly I can separate it out. It’s all a bit tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about lots of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lots of different things,” exactly. I do worry, but then I get stuck trying to decide whether it’s excessive or just… being a person with responsibilities.\n\nLike, I can worry about practical things, and then it sort of spirals into remembering something completely unrelated—like I’ll think about paying a bill and then suddenly I’m wondering whether I replied to someone’s message from three days ago. But I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m worrying about everything.” It’s more… bits and pieces, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if I’d say “many different parts.” I mean, I do worry, but then I start making categories in my head, which is probably not what you’re asking. Work sort of leaks into everything, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m worrying about life in general or just one thing wearing different hats.\n\nLike, I’ll be thinking about something ordinary—shopping, or whether I’ve got clean clothes for tomorrow—and then somehow it becomes connected to school, because I need to remember something for a lesson, or I’ve forgotten to print something, and then I’m off on that track. But I don’t sit there with a list of worries about every area of my life, if that makes sense. I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: During these past year and seven months, have you felt down or low nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can honestly say nearly every day, because then I start thinking, well, what counts as “down”? Like, is it the whole day, or waking up with that sort of grey feeling, or just dipping in and out?\n\nIt has been around a lot, I think. But I haven’t been marking it on a calendar or anything. And the “past year and seven months” is such a specific stretch that my brain immediately wants to break it into terms and holidays and exam periods, which probably isn’t helpful. I can say it’s felt frequent, but I’m not sure I could confidently say nearly every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things sometimes, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m interested. But it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and messing about in the garden—there was always something to check on, or weed, or decide whether I’d planted too many tomatoes again. And I help with the robotics club at school too, which used to be one of those things that made the week feel a bit brighter.\n\nNow I mostly just… go through the motions. Or I cancel. I’ll tell myself I’m too busy, or that someone else can cover it, but if I’m honest, it’s more that none of it feels enjoyable in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt down or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: Two years… I’m not sure I can pin it down that neatly. I keep trying to line it up with school years, because that’s how I remember time—autumn term, mock exams, summer holidays—and then it gets a bit muddled.\n\nI have felt low a lot, but “most of the time” over two whole years feels hard to answer. Some stretches blur together, and then there are bits where I was just busy or distracted, or I don’t know if I’d have called it depressed exactly. So… I don’t think I can say for certain.\n\nDoctor: If the school workload were no longer a problem, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Part of me wants to say yes, because the workload is such an obvious thing to blame, isn’t it? Like marking piles up and suddenly everything feels impossible.\n\nBut then… I don’t know. Even in lighter weeks, I can’t always tell whether I actually feel better or whether I’m just less cornered. It’s hard to separate it out. Teaching kind of leaks into everything—weekends, evenings, even when I’m buying milk I’m thinking about whether Year 10 understood the lesson.\n\nSo if the workload disappeared completely… I don’t know. Maybe I’d feel better. Maybe I’d still feel low and just have fewer excuses for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep’s been pretty rubbish, actually. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open while I’m brushing my teeth, and then as soon as I’m in bed my brain starts doing this awful replay of everything.\n\nIt’ll be assessment deadlines, or some tiny mistake I made in class, or whether I handled something badly with a pupil—things that probably no one else is even thinking about. And then I’ll wake up around 4 in the morning and just… lie there. Sometimes I doze a bit, but I don’t really get properly back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I decided to eat less, or anything like that. It’s more that on school days I’ll get to, I don’t know, three or four o’clock and realise I’ve only had coffee or maybe half a cereal bar that was in my bag.\n\nSometimes I tell myself I’ll eat at break, and then there’s a student at the door, or someone needs the photocopier code, or I’m trying to mark something quickly, and then lunch just sort of… disappears. Our staffroom fridge is also disgusting, which doesn’t help—people leave yoghurts in there for weeks.\n\nBut yes, my appetite’s definitely changed. I’m not really trying to lose weight, but over the last year my clothes have got noticeably looser. At first I thought maybe things had stretched in the wash, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but… yeah, it’s enough that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… quite a lot, actually. It’s embarrassing because teaching is basically being able to make decisions every thirty seconds, isn’t it? And I used to be quick with marking, not necessarily cheerful about it, but quick.\n\nNow I’ll read the same student answer three or four times and still sit there thinking, “What mark is that?” Like I can see the words, but they don’t… settle into meaning properly. And then I start second-guessing the mark scheme, and whether I’m being fair, and whether I’m too harsh on that particular class because they were awful after lunch on Tuesdays—sorry, that’s probably not relevant.\n\nEven simple things can take forever. I had to order lab supplies the other day—just basic stuff—and I got completely stuck choosing what to get. I think I spent nearly an hour going back and forth over it, which is ridiculous. It’s like my brain just won’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m speaking slowly or anything.\n\nMoving slowly… I mean, mornings at school can feel like wading through treacle, but that might just be because the photocopier jams every other day and Year 9 always seem to appear before I’ve even taken my coat off. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI suppose I haven’t noticed myself being noticeably slower in a way other people would point out. I might feel slow inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I look or sound slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or drained of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By the time I’ve got through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting, I feel completely wrung out. Like my body’s heavy, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’ll get home and even making dinner feels like this huge thing. Or I’ll open my laptop to answer a few parent emails and it feels like climbing a hill. Which is silly, because it’s just typing, but I just feel so drained.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But I don’t sit there with a list of worries about every area of my life, if that makes sense. I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: During these past year and seven months, have you felt down or low nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can honestly say nearly every day, because then I start thinking, well, what counts as “down”? Like, is it the whole day, or waking up with that sort of grey feeling, or just dipping in and out?\n\nIt has been around a lot, I think. But I haven’t been marking it on a calendar or anything. And the “past year and seven months” is such a specific stretch that my brain immediately wants to break it into terms and holidays and exam periods, which probably isn’t helpful. I can say it’s felt frequent, but I’m not sure I could confidently say nearly every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things sometimes, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m interested. But it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and messing about in the garden—there was always something to check on, or weed, or decide whether I’d planted too many tomatoes again. And I help with the robotics club at school too, which used to be one of those things that made the week feel a bit brighter.\n\nNow I mostly just… go through the motions. Or I cancel. I’ll tell myself I’m too busy, or that someone else can cover it, but if I’m honest, it’s more that none of it feels enjoyable in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt down or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: Two years… I’m not sure I can pin it down that neatly. I keep trying to line it up with school years, because that’s how I remember time—autumn term, mock exams, summer holidays—and then it gets a bit muddled.\n\nI have felt low a lot, but “most of the time” over two whole years feels hard to answer. Some stretches blur together, and then there are bits where I was just busy or distracted, or I don’t know if I’d have called it depressed exactly. So… I don’t think I can say for certain.\n\nDoctor: If the school workload were no longer a problem, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Part of me wants to say yes, because the workload is such an obvious thing to blame, isn’t it? Like marking piles up and suddenly everything feels impossible.\n\nBut then… I don’t know. Even in lighter weeks, I can’t always tell whether I actually feel better or whether I’m just less cornered. It’s hard to separate it out. Teaching kind of leaks into everything—weekends, evenings, even when I’m buying milk I’m thinking about whether Year 10 understood the lesson.\n\nSo if the workload disappeared completely… I don’t know. Maybe I’d feel better. Maybe I’d still feel low and just have fewer excuses for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep’s been pretty rubbish, actually. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open while I’m brushing my teeth, and then as soon as I’m in bed my brain starts doing this awful replay of everything.\n\nIt’ll be assessment deadlines, or some tiny mistake I made in class, or whether I handled something badly with a pupil—things that probably no one else is even thinking about. And then I’ll wake up around 4 in the morning and just… lie there. Sometimes I doze a bit, but I don’t really get properly back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I decided to eat less, or anything like that. It’s more that on school days I’ll get to, I don’t know, three or four o’clock and realise I’ve only had coffee or maybe half a cereal bar that was in my bag.\n\nSometimes I tell myself I’ll eat at break, and then there’s a student at the door, or someone needs the photocopier code, or I’m trying to mark something quickly, and then lunch just sort of… disappears. Our staffroom fridge is also disgusting, which doesn’t help—people leave yoghurts in there for weeks.\n\nBut yes, my appetite’s definitely changed. I’m not really trying to lose weight, but over the last year my clothes have got noticeably looser. At first I thought maybe things had stretched in the wash, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but… yeah, it’s enough that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… quite a lot, actually. It’s embarrassing because teaching is basically being able to make decisions every thirty seconds, isn’t it? And I used to be quick with marking, not necessarily cheerful about it, but quick.\n\nNow I’ll read the same student answer three or four times and still sit there thinking, “What mark is that?” Like I can see the words, but they don’t… settle into meaning properly. And then I start second-guessing the mark scheme, and whether I’m being fair, and whether I’m too harsh on that particular class because they were awful after lunch on Tuesdays—sorry, that’s probably not relevant.\n\nEven simple things can take forever. I had to order lab supplies the other day—just basic stuff—and I got completely stuck choosing what to get. I think I spent nearly an hour going back and forth over it, which is ridiculous. It’s like my brain just won’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m speaking slowly or anything.\n\nMoving slowly… I mean, mornings at school can feel like wading through treacle, but that might just be because the photocopier jams every other day and Year 9 always seem to appear before I’ve even taken my coat off. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI suppose I haven’t noticed myself being noticeably slower in a way other people would point out. I might feel slow inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I look or sound slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or drained of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By the time I’ve got through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting, I feel completely wrung out. Like my body’s heavy, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’ll get home and even making dinner feels like this huge thing. Or I’ll open my laptop to answer a few parent emails and it feels like climbing a hill. Which is silly, because it’s just typing, but I just feel so drained.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes. I mean… I’m still going in, so part of me keeps thinking, well, it can’t be that bad, can it? But it is affecting things.

Work sort of leaks into everything, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m worrying about life in general or just one thing wearing different hats.\n\nLike, I’ll be thinking about something ordinary—shopping, or whether I’ve got clean clothes for tomorrow—and then somehow it becomes connected to school, because I need to remember something for a lesson, or I’ve forgotten to print something, and then I’m off on that track. But I don’t sit there with a list of worries about every area of my life, if that makes sense. I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: During these past year and seven months, have you felt down or low nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can honestly say nearly every day, because then I start thinking, well, what counts as “down”? And the “past year and seven months” is such a specific stretch that my brain immediately wants to break it into terms and holidays and exam periods, which probably isn’t helpful. I can say it’s felt frequent, but I’m not sure I could confidently say nearly every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things sometimes, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m interested. And I help with the robotics club at school too, which used to be one of those things that made the week feel a bit brighter.\n\nNow I mostly just… go through the motions. Or I cancel. I keep trying to line it up with school years, because that’s how I remember time—autumn term, mock exams, summer holidays—and then it gets a bit muddled.\n\nI have felt low a lot, but “most of the time” over two whole years feels hard to answer. Some stretches blur together, and then there are bits where I was just busy or distracted, or I don’t know if I’d have called it depressed exactly. So… I don’t think I can say for certain.\n\nDoctor: If the school workload were no longer a problem, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Part of me wants to say yes, because the workload is such an obvious thing to blame, isn’t it? Like marking piles up and suddenly everything feels impossible.\n\nBut then… I don’t know. Even in lighter weeks, I can’t always tell whether I actually feel better or whether I’m just less cornered. It’s hard to separate it out. Teaching kind of leaks into everything—weekends, evenings, even when I’m buying milk I’m thinking about whether Year 10 understood the lesson.\n\nSo if the workload disappeared completely… I don’t know. Maybe I’d feel better. Maybe I’d still feel low and just have fewer excuses for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep’s been pretty rubbish, actually. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open while I’m brushing my teeth, and then as soon as I’m in bed my brain starts doing this awful replay of everything.\n\nIt’ll be assessment deadlines, or some tiny mistake I made in class, or whether I handled something badly with a pupil—things that probably no one else is even thinking about. And then I’ll wake up around 4 in the morning and just… lie there. Sometimes I doze a bit, but I don’t really get properly back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I decided to eat less, or anything like that. It’s more that on school days I’ll get to, I don’t know, three or four o’clock and realise I’ve only had coffee or maybe half a cereal bar that was in my bag.\n\nSometimes I tell myself I’ll eat at break, and then there’s a student at the door, or someone needs the photocopier code, or I’m trying to mark something quickly, and then lunch just sort of… disappears. Our staffroom fridge is also disgusting, which doesn’t help—people leave yoghurts in there for weeks.\n\nBut yes, my appetite’s definitely changed. I’m not really trying to lose weight, but over the last year my clothes have got noticeably looser. At first I thought maybe things had stretched in the wash, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but… yeah, it’s enough that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… quite a lot, actually. It’s embarrassing because teaching is basically being able to make decisions every thirty seconds, isn’t it? And I used to be quick with marking, not necessarily cheerful about it, but quick.\n\nNow I’ll read the same student answer three or four times and still sit there thinking, “What mark is that?” Like I can see the words, but they don’t… settle into meaning properly. And then I start second-guessing the mark scheme, and whether I’m being fair, and whether I’m too harsh on that particular class because they were awful after lunch on Tuesdays—sorry, that’s probably not relevant.\n\nEven simple things can take forever. I had to order lab supplies the other day—just basic stuff—and I got completely stuck choosing what to get. I think I spent nearly an hour going back and forth over it, which is ridiculous. It’s like my brain just won’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m speaking slowly or anything.\n\nMoving slowly… I mean, mornings at school can feel like wading through treacle, but that might just be because the photocopier jams every other day and Year 9 always seem to appear before I’ve even taken my coat off. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI suppose I haven’t noticed myself being noticeably slower in a way other people would point out. I might feel slow inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I look or sound slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or drained of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By the time I’ve got through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting, I feel completely wrung out. Like my body’s heavy, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’ll get home and even making dinner feels like this huge thing. Or I’ll open my laptop to answer a few parent emails and it feels like climbing a hill. Which is silly, because it’s just typing, but I just feel so drained.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I’m still going in, so part of me keeps thinking, well, it can’t be that bad, can it? But it is affecting things.\n\nI’m falling behind on marking and lesson planning, and that does spill into the classroom. I hate that. I used to be really meticulous with grades, probably annoyingly so, and recently I made a mistake with one and it really scared me. Not scared like dramatic, just… it felt unlike me, and I kept thinking about the student and whether I’d let them down.\n\nAnd at home I’m not really… showing up, I suppose. Friends message and I either don’t reply or I say I’m busy, and family things feel like too much. My life has got very small: get through school, come home exhausted, then somehow do it again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I don’t want to do anything, I don’t want to act on it, but having those thoughts at all really frightened me.\n\nDoctor: Did these thoughts and the way you’ve been feeling start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start around a particular change. But I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m worrying about everything.” It’s more… bits and pieces, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if I’d say “many different parts.” I mean, I do worry, but then I start making categories in my head, which is probably not what you’re asking. Work sort of leaks into everything, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m worrying about life in general or just one thing wearing different hats.\n\nLike, I’ll be thinking about something ordinary—shopping, or whether I’ve got clean clothes for tomorrow—and then somehow it becomes connected to school, because I need to remember something for a lesson, or I’ve forgotten to print something, and then I’m off on that track. My life has got very small: get through school, come home exhausted, then somehow do it again.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhat is the longest stretch of time you've felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about 1 year and 7 months, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The longest stretch… I think it’s been about a year and seven months now. It sort of started when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load.

I remember thinking at first, “Well, it’s just a busy term,” because in teaching everything gets blamed on the term, doesn’t it? There’s always some deadline or data drop or someone needing a spreadsheet in a slightly different colour. But it didn’t really lift after that. It’s been most days, most of the day, for… yeah, about a year and seven months.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F32.9, F43.2, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I… um, I’m not even sure how to start. I guess I’m here because I scared myself. I’m a teacher, and usually I can kind of keep it together for the kids—well, my students, I mean, they notice everything, honestly—but lately on the drive home I’ve caught myself thinking that maybe everyone would be better off without me.\n\nAnd sometimes at night I think it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. I don’t want to do anything, I don’t want to act on it, but having those thoughts at all really frightened me.\n\nDoctor: Did these thoughts and the way you’ve been feeling start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start around a particular change. It’s been about… a year and seven months now, I think. Around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it like that, because it’s just curriculum and spreadsheets and meetings, but there were so many extra pieces—like people needing updates, and everyone having opinions, and I’d be trying to grade after dinner and then remember some rubric thing at midnight. Sorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, that’s when it started, or at least when I really noticed it beginning.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you felt this way most days for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, definitely. It’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, since I took over that STEM assessment redesign along with my usual teaching load. I keep thinking, “Oh, it’ll settle after this deadline,” but then there’s another meeting or another set of spreadsheets, and somehow it’s just… kept going.\n\nDoctor: If that school project were taken off your plate, do you think you would still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I keep trying to imagine that, like if someone just took the whole binder and the meetings and all the tracking sheets away, whether I’d sort of… snap back.\n\nPart of me thinks I’d feel lighter, obviously. But I’m not sure it would all just disappear. It’s hard to separate it now, because it’s been tied to my whole routine for so long. Even on weekends or breaks, I catch myself still feeling like something is hanging over me. But then again, teachers are terrible at resting—at least the ones I know. We’ll get a day off and spend it reorganizing cupboards or answering emails we swore we wouldn’t answer.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I think it might help, but I don’t know if it would fix everything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from school responsibilities for a while, do you still feel this low?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, when I’m physically away from school—like not in the building, not doing meetings—it doesn’t always switch off in my head.\n\nBut then sometimes I wonder if I’m just bad at being “away,” because I’ll be at home and notice the tote bag with papers in it, or I’ll remember some email I haven’t answered, and then suddenly it’s like I’m back there anyway. My partner says teachers have a weird definition of time off, which is probably true… we had a whole conversation once about how summer “break” is not really a break, and then I started making a list of classroom supplies, which kind of proved the point.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can say clearly. I think I can still feel low even when I’m not actively doing school stuff, but I’m not sure how much of that is because it’s still in the background.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like during uni when everything felt dramatic and impossible for about five minutes—well, it probably wasn’t five minutes, but you know what I mean. And teaching has never exactly been a calm profession, so there have definitely been times I’ve felt really worn down.\n\nBut like this? I’m not sure. I can’t think of another time that stands out clearly in the same way. It’s hard because you look back and everything gets sort of blurry, and I start comparing it to exam periods or family stress or my first year teaching, which was its own little disaster. So… I don’t know if I’d say I had another period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing school-related is happening, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I want to say yes, sometimes, but then I start picking apart whether “nothing school-related” ever really happens, because my brain will make something school-related out of almost anything. Like I’ll be watching some completely unrelated programme and suddenly think, “Oh, that would make a good example for Year 10,” and then there I am again.\n\nBut if you mean, like, if I’m just sitting there and no one’s emailed me and I’m not planning anything… I think I can still feel low. I just don’t know how cleanly I can separate it out. It’s all a bit tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about lots of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lots of different things,” exactly. I do worry, but then I get stuck trying to decide whether it’s excessive or just… being a person with responsibilities.\n\nLike, I can worry about practical things, and then it sort of spirals into remembering something completely unrelated—like I’ll think about paying a bill and then suddenly I’m wondering whether I replied to someone’s message from three days ago. But I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m worrying about everything.” It’s more… bits and pieces, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if I’d say “many different parts.” I mean, I do worry, but then I start making categories in my head, which is probably not what you’re asking. Work sort of leaks into everything, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m worrying about life in general or just one thing wearing different hats.\n\nLike, I’ll be thinking about something ordinary—shopping, or whether I’ve got clean clothes for tomorrow—and then somehow it becomes connected to school, because I need to remember something for a lesson, or I’ve forgotten to print something, and then I’m off on that track. But I don’t sit there with a list of worries about every area of my life, if that makes sense. I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: During these past year and seven months, have you felt down or low nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can honestly say nearly every day, because then I start thinking, well, what counts as “down”? Like, is it the whole day, or waking up with that sort of grey feeling, or just dipping in and out?\n\nIt has been around a lot, I think. But I haven’t been marking it on a calendar or anything. And the “past year and seven months” is such a specific stretch that my brain immediately wants to break it into terms and holidays and exam periods, which probably isn’t helpful. I can say it’s felt frequent, but I’m not sure I could confidently say nearly every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things sometimes, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m interested. But it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and messing about in the garden—there was always something to check on, or weed, or decide whether I’d planted too many tomatoes again. And I help with the robotics club at school too, which used to be one of those things that made the week feel a bit brighter.\n\nNow I mostly just… go through the motions. Or I cancel. I’ll tell myself I’m too busy, or that someone else can cover it, but if I’m honest, it’s more that none of it feels enjoyable in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt down or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: Two years… I’m not sure I can pin it down that neatly. I keep trying to line it up with school years, because that’s how I remember time—autumn term, mock exams, summer holidays—and then it gets a bit muddled.\n\nI have felt low a lot, but “most of the time” over two whole years feels hard to answer. Some stretches blur together, and then there are bits where I was just busy or distracted, or I don’t know if I’d have called it depressed exactly. So… I don’t think I can say for certain.\n\nDoctor: If the school workload were no longer a problem, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Part of me wants to say yes, because the workload is such an obvious thing to blame, isn’t it? Like marking piles up and suddenly everything feels impossible.\n\nBut then… I don’t know. Even in lighter weeks, I can’t always tell whether I actually feel better or whether I’m just less cornered. It’s hard to separate it out. Teaching kind of leaks into everything—weekends, evenings, even when I’m buying milk I’m thinking about whether Year 10 understood the lesson.\n\nSo if the workload disappeared completely… I don’t know. Maybe I’d feel better. Maybe I’d still feel low and just have fewer excuses for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep’s been pretty rubbish, actually. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open while I’m brushing my teeth, and then as soon as I’m in bed my brain starts doing this awful replay of everything.\n\nIt’ll be assessment deadlines, or some tiny mistake I made in class, or whether I handled something badly with a pupil—things that probably no one else is even thinking about. And then I’ll wake up around 4 in the morning and just… lie there. Sometimes I doze a bit, but I don’t really get properly back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I decided to eat less, or anything like that. It’s more that on school days I’ll get to, I don’t know, three or four o’clock and realise I’ve only had coffee or maybe half a cereal bar that was in my bag.\n\nSometimes I tell myself I’ll eat at break, and then there’s a student at the door, or someone needs the photocopier code, or I’m trying to mark something quickly, and then lunch just sort of… disappears. Our staffroom fridge is also disgusting, which doesn’t help—people leave yoghurts in there for weeks.\n\nBut yes, my appetite’s definitely changed. I’m not really trying to lose weight, but over the last year my clothes have got noticeably looser. At first I thought maybe things had stretched in the wash, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but… yeah, it’s enough that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… quite a lot, actually. It’s embarrassing because teaching is basically being able to make decisions every thirty seconds, isn’t it? And I used to be quick with marking, not necessarily cheerful about it, but quick.\n\nNow I’ll read the same student answer three or four times and still sit there thinking, “What mark is that?” Like I can see the words, but they don’t… settle into meaning properly. And then I start second-guessing the mark scheme, and whether I’m being fair, and whether I’m too harsh on that particular class because they were awful after lunch on Tuesdays—sorry, that’s probably not relevant.\n\nEven simple things can take forever. I had to order lab supplies the other day—just basic stuff—and I got completely stuck choosing what to get. I think I spent nearly an hour going back and forth over it, which is ridiculous. It’s like my brain just won’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m speaking slowly or anything.\n\nMoving slowly… I mean, mornings at school can feel like wading through treacle, but that might just be because the photocopier jams every other day and Year 9 always seem to appear before I’ve even taken my coat off. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI suppose I haven’t noticed myself being noticeably slower in a way other people would point out. I might feel slow inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I look or sound slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or drained of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By the time I’ve got through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting, I feel completely wrung out. Like my body’s heavy, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’ll get home and even making dinner feels like this huge thing. Or I’ll open my laptop to answer a few parent emails and it feels like climbing a hill. Which is silly, because it’s just typing, but I just feel so drained.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I’m still going in, so part of me keeps thinking, well, it can’t be that bad, can it? But it is affecting things.\n\nI’m falling behind on marking and lesson planning, and that does spill into the classroom. I hate that. I used to be really meticulous with grades, probably annoyingly so, and recently I made a mistake with one and it really scared me. Not scared like dramatic, just… it felt unlike me, and I kept thinking about the student and whether I’d let them down.\n\nAnd at home I’m not really… showing up, I suppose. Friends message and I either don’t reply or I say I’m busy, and family things feel like too much. My life has got very small: get through school, come home exhausted, then somehow do it again.\n\nDoctor: What is the longest stretch of time you've felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: The longest stretch… I think it’s been about a year and seven months now. It sort of started when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nI remember thinking at first, “Well, it’s just a busy term,” because in teaching everything gets blamed on the term, doesn’t it? Teaching kind of leaks into everything—weekends, evenings, even when I’m buying milk I’m thinking about whether Year 10 understood the lesson.\n\nSo if the workload disappeared completely… I don’t know. Maybe I’d feel better. Maybe I’d still feel low and just have fewer excuses for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep’s been pretty rubbish, actually. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open while I’m brushing my teeth, and then as soon as I’m in bed my brain starts doing this awful replay of everything.\n\nIt’ll be assessment deadlines, or some tiny mistake I made in class, or whether I handled something badly with a pupil—things that probably no one else is even thinking about. And then I’ll wake up around 4 in the morning and just… lie there. Sometimes I doze a bit, but I don’t really get properly back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I decided to eat less, or anything like that. It’s more that on school days I’ll get to, I don’t know, three or four o’clock and realise I’ve only had coffee or maybe half a cereal bar that was in my bag.\n\nSometimes I tell myself I’ll eat at break, and then there’s a student at the door, or someone needs the photocopier code, or I’m trying to mark something quickly, and then lunch just sort of… disappears. Our staffroom fridge is also disgusting, which doesn’t help—people leave yoghurts in there for weeks.\n\nBut yes, my appetite’s definitely changed. I’m not really trying to lose weight, but over the last year my clothes have got noticeably looser. At first I thought maybe things had stretched in the wash, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but… yeah, it’s enough that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… quite a lot, actually. It’s embarrassing because teaching is basically being able to make decisions every thirty seconds, isn’t it? And I used to be quick with marking, not necessarily cheerful about it, but quick.\n\nNow I’ll read the same student answer three or four times and still sit there thinking, “What mark is that?” Like I can see the words, but they don’t… settle into meaning properly. And then I start second-guessing the mark scheme, and whether I’m being fair, and whether I’m too harsh on that particular class because they were awful after lunch on Tuesdays—sorry, that’s probably not relevant.\n\nEven simple things can take forever. I had to order lab supplies the other day—just basic stuff—and I got completely stuck choosing what to get. I think I spent nearly an hour going back and forth over it, which is ridiculous. It’s like my brain just won’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m speaking slowly or anything.\n\nMoving slowly… I mean, mornings at school can feel like wading through treacle, but that might just be because the photocopier jams every other day and Year 9 always seem to appear before I’ve even taken my coat off. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI suppose I haven’t noticed myself being noticeably slower in a way other people would point out. I might feel slow inside, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I look or sound slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or drained of energy?\n\nPatient: Yes. By the time I’ve got through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting, I feel completely wrung out. Like my body’s heavy, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’ll get home and even making dinner feels like this huge thing. Or I’ll open my laptop to answer a few parent emails and it feels like climbing a hill. Which is silly, because it’s just typing, but I just feel so drained.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I’m still going in, so part of me keeps thinking, well, it can’t be that bad, can it? But it is affecting things.\n\nI’m falling behind on marking and lesson planning, and that does spill into the classroom. I hate that. I used to be really meticulous with grades, probably annoyingly so, and recently I made a mistake with one and it really scared me. Not scared like dramatic, just… it felt unlike me, and I kept thinking about the student and whether I’d let them down.\n\nAnd at home I’m not really… showing up, I suppose. Friends message and I either don’t reply or I say I’m busy, and family things feel like too much. My life has got very small: get through school, come home exhausted, then somehow do it again.\n\nDoctor: What is the longest stretch of time you've felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: The longest stretch… I think it’s been about a year and seven months now. It sort of started when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nI remember thinking at first, “Well, it’s just a busy term,” because in teaching everything gets blamed on the term, doesn’t it? There’s always some deadline or data drop or someone needing a spreadsheet in a slightly different colour. But it didn’t really lift after that. It’s been most days, most of the day, for… yeah, about a year and seven months."
